Qa8# { 1-0 Stockfish-Dragon 3.3 by Komodo Chess,6 26L-WhiteBlitz-Slav-Lichess-Slav-Video-4 2024 (63) }) 6. e4 b5 { This is a typical scenario in Slav/Semi-Slav systems. White sacrifices the c4-pawn and Black plays ...b5 to protect it and consolidate his extra pawn. This looks a little scary for White: that queenside pawn majority looks pretty huge, the d3-square is taken away from the white pieces and Black has the idea of ...b4 to drive the white knight on c3 away from the defence of e4. Moreover, if Black manages to trade the c6-pawn for the d4-pawn with ...c5, then White will have little to show for the pawn deficit. White's compensation lies in a range of factors: 1. The double pawn centre which gives White a space advantage and flexibility in possible central pushes: d5 or e5 2. The ability to drive away a black piece from the key kingside defensive square f6 3. A slight lead in development 4. The fact that Black's counterplay against the centre (...c5) takes a while to organise. White has a wide choice of development ideas. Leela WDL Contempts's suggestion was very interesting; to fianchetto the light-squared bishop with g3 and Bg2 and turn this into some sort of sharp Catalan! From g2, the light-squared bishop is of course not pointing towards a black king castled on the kingside, but it does support the white central pawns which makes d5 much more likely in response to Black's future ...c5. Putting the bishop on g2 also leaves the d- and e-files free for the active development of White's queens and rooks. White's development is a kind of blended solution in which all manner of central squares are supported / attacked giving all sorts of possibilities for the future without carrying a concrete threat right now. } 7. g3 e6 { The most natural move and the only response I have faced at the time of writing (12-4-2024). There are however other sensible possibilities. } (7... b4 { If you want to use the disruptive qualities of Black's queenside pawn majority, then it's best to do so now before White develops the bishop to g2. } 8. Na4 Nxe4 9. Bxb8 { Not a move that would have occurred to me, but the only engine choice. This weakens Black's control of c6 (handy for attack with Ne5 and Bg2) while dragging the black rook to an exposed square (open to forks by Nxc6) } 9... Rxb8 10. Ne5 { [#] } 10... Qd6 { [#] White has 2 promising alternatives } 11. Bxc4 (11. Bg2 f5 12. f3 Nf6 13. Nxc4 Qd5 14. Rc1 g6 15. O-O Bh6 16. f4 Ne4 17. Ncb6 (17. Nc5 Bg7 18. Ne5 Bxe5 19. fxe5 O-O 20. b3 Qd8 21. Ng5 { is the engine recommendation. 13.Nxd5 cxd5 14.Ng5 may be easier on the memory for a blitz game. On the one hand, you give Black fewer choices (so less uncertainty) but you do give yourself an easier ride to the typical attacking structure you want. } 13... h6 { The most natural human move in my opinion, dealing with amy Qc2 ideas from White (forcing Black to weaken the kinsgide with ...g6) while looking for early clarity on where the knight will retreat to (h3 or e4) } (13... c5 { A popular reaction, played twice in my games but losing to an easy tactic. It's obviously very natural and thematic to strike at the white centre and open the a8-h1 diagonal for the passive bishop on b7. Black's central light squares look strong, but remove the support of the c6-pawn and open the long diagonal for the white bishop on g2 and suddenly they are lacking protection. The knight on g5 also attacks e6 and f7 as well as h7! It shows the advantage of giving Black a choice at move 13 rather than forcing a structure with 13.Nxd5 } 14. Nh3 { This was part of Leela WDL Contempt's main line when suggesting this system against the 4...a6 Slav. What attracted Leela to the position? On the one hand, Black seems to have emerged pretty well from the opening: an extra pawn and an advanced queenside pawn majority with no huge structural weaknesses and a reasonable state of development. Moreover, by exchanging knights on d5 White has brought a black pawn to d5 which takes away e4 as a transit point for White's pieces (light-squared bishop and rook) on the way to Black's kingside. White is reasoning that although Black's position looks nice, Black's pieces are somewhat blocked behind Black's monolithic pawn structure. It's not particularly easy for Black to "get at" the white position in the next few moves. From the white side however, White has a typical attacking pawn structure with a pawn on e5 giving White a kingside space advanatge and preventing any black minor piece from defending the kingside from f6. We also have a little "hook" on h6 which we can attack together with the dark-squared bishop either with Qg4 or Qd2. It's not huge, but it's a start and the fact that Black's counterplay will arrive very slowly gives White lots of other opportunities to redeploy pieces, of which the most common idea is the redeployment of the light-squared bishop to the b1-h7 diagonal with Bf3-d1-c2 and the kingside push g4-g5. I have faced 3 moves in my blitz games: 15...f6, 15...Re8 and 15...b4. In terms of natural moves / thinkable human ideas, I would also add 15...f5 as well as 15...a5 with the idea of protecting e6 with ...Ra6 to allow ...f5 later. 15...Qe8 is also often a useful defensive move though it looks odd to play it here. One final point to mention is psychological. From a human point of view, Nxd5 is unexpected. Black may get a feeling that he's been tricked and he could have avoided this by taking on f4 or c3 a move earlier. Judging from the time players spend recapturing on d5 (after 14.Nxd5) the feeling of having missed a trick and fallen into something may well enter Black's mind at this point. } (15. Terrorising White in the 3.Bb5 Sicilian!

[Event "Leela WDL Contempt"] [Date "2024.04.20"] [White "Sicilian 3.Bb5 g5"] [Black "1.e4"] [Result "*"] [Annotator "gmmat"] [Variant "Standard"] [ECO "B30"] [Opening "Sicilian Defense: Nyezhmetdinov-Rossolimo Attack"] [StudyName "GM Matthew Sadler - Silicon Road YouTube Channel additional material"] [ChapterName "Crazy Leela: Terrorising White in the 3.Bb5 Sicilian!"] [ChapterURL "https://lichess.org/study/GJ5NRo9a/7w9m5OBu"] 1. e4 c5 2. Nf3 Nc6 3. Bb5 g5 { This idea was highlighted by TCEC chatter mrbdzz when he ran his Novelty Grinder tool (https://matthewsadler.me.uk/openings/the-mrbdzz-novelty-grinder/) on the Candidates games. Ever one for a challenge, I started playing it in all my Black games! Obviously 3...g5 gives away a pawn for seemingly not very much, but looks can be deceptive. The source of compensation is White's 3rd move 3.Bb5 which has weakened the kingside light squares by unprotecting g2. Opening up the g-file - in accelerated fashion by simply giving away the g-pawn - to then target this pawn with a rook is a logical (if rather extreme!) response. The other tactical point is the possible gain of tempo with ...Nd4, hitting the bishop on b5 and attacking the kingside light squares e2 and f3. Finally, the ...d5 break is available to Black to hit e4 and get the black queen to the powerful central light square d5 after exd5. } 4. Nxg5 (4. Sicilian correction: Kan Sicilian
